# 6. Mon Dieu

[Mon Dieu — Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/mon-dieu/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

**Serial:** 106  
**Date:** 12 April 2019  
**Category:** “Some serious essays and thoughts on religion and how we can be united.”  
**Dedication:** God  
**Collection:** *Poetrusic by Chaos*  
**Work:** *Infused :: Inspired to Love*  
**Rhymes:** Alternate ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/mon-dieu/))

This is a significant jump from **#5 *Wake Up***.

If *Wake Up* asks humanity to awaken to the Divine, *Mon Dieu* asks a much more intimate question:

> **“If God had a name what would it be?”**

And then almost immediately refuses to let God remain safely somewhere “out there”:

> **“Look into your heart, open eye and see / That God lives in every last Earthly place”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/mon-dieu/))

That is the centre of this poem.

---

# Praise

What I particularly like here is the **scale of the Divine**.

God is simultaneously:

- in the human heart,
- in every earthly place,
- on the dark side of the moon,
- beyond Orion and Lyra,
- in nature,
- in the “Kernel of Bliss,”
- and ultimately at the gate awaiting us.

The poem refuses to give the Divine only one address.

That matters enormously for the **spiritual-democracy** thread we're tracing.

You ask *what would you call God?*-but the poem's answer is effectively:

**Before you name Him, look.**

And then:

**Look everywhere.**

---

# Context

There is a fascinating shift in the metadata itself.

The earlier pieces we've examined were in *Spontaneous Love*. Here, by April 2019, you've moved into **Poetrusic by Chaos**, while retaining *Infused :: Inspired to Love*. The dedication has also become simply:

**God.** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/mon-dieu/))

And the page's own introductory description is unusually revealing:

> “About that cosmic divine.”

It then names:

**Kala. Bhairava. Anandamayakosha.**

So the poem is explicitly entering Indic metaphysical territory-but it doesn't stay there.

It asks:

> “Was He an alien of the race of the Devas”

and:

> “Was He a symbol of dynamism and timelessness”

Then it moves through **Bhakta, Divine Ladder, Kingdom of Heaven, Mother Nature, immortality**.

Again, the religious vocabulary is deliberately porous.

Christian language and Hindu/Yogic language are not being made to fight for territory.

They are being allowed to **describe the same mystery from different angles**.

That is one of the strongest examples so far of what your pluralism looks like *inside the poetry itself*.

---

# 3 Quotes

### 1. God beyond naming

> “If God had a name what would it be?”

The question itself is almost more important than any answer.

A name can reveal something about God-but it can also limit our conception of God.

### 2. Divine immanence

> “That God lives in every last Earthly place”

This is probably the poem's most important theological statement.

The Divine isn't confined to sacred buildings, scriptures, heavens or particular communities.

### 3. The Divine within

> “For finally in our hearts he's come to stay”

The poem eventually travels the enormous distance from **cosmos → nature → myth → heaven → the human heart**.

And it ends with indwelling.

([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/mon-dieu/))

---

# 3 Places Where Those of Differing Views May Oppose

## 1. The poem assumes God

An atheist or strict materialist can quite reasonably object that the poem treats God's existence as an experiential truth rather than a proposition requiring proof.

And the poem doesn't really try to prove God.

It does something different:

**It invites the reader to look.**

That will be persuasive to someone open to spiritual experience and unpersuasive to someone who demands empirical demonstration.

That's an important distinction.

---

## 2. “The Devil in his dark tower” introduces moral dualism

The poem warns of those who:

> “worship the Devil in his dark tower”

and contrasts this with grace and the Divine.

A reader from a non-dualistic spiritual tradition could find this problematic.

The danger is that “evil” can become externalised into *those people over there*.

But there's another reading available within the poem: the “Devil” represents the rejection of grace, the abandonment of the needy, and the failure to tend one's inner light.

That makes the battle principally **within consciousness**, rather than a licence to demonise other human beings.

---

## 3. “Kingdom of Heaven” and immortality may be read too literally

The poem speaks of:

> “The Kingdom of Heaven”

and later:

> “win immortality”

A secular reader could regard these as metaphysical claims without evidence.

Even another spiritual reader might interpret them differently-perhaps psychologically, symbolically or evolutionarily.

And that is actually useful for spiritual democracy: **the shared aspiration doesn't require everyone to use exactly the same metaphysical vocabulary.**

---

# What the Message of Integration May Be

I think *Mon Dieu* gives us perhaps the clearest statement yet of the principle underlying these poems:

## **The Divine cannot be monopolised by a name.**

Look at the poem's movement:

**God**  
→ **Earth**  
→ **Moon**  
→ **Orion**  
→ **Lyra**  
→ **Devas**  
→ **Kala**  
→ **Bhairava**  
→ **Bhakta**  
→ **Kingdom of Heaven**  
→ **Mother Nature**  
→ **human heart**

That's an extraordinary religious map.

And importantly, the poem doesn't say:

**“These are twelve different gods.”**

Nor does it say:

**“These are all literally identical.”**

It lets the symbols coexist around a central mystery.

That is much closer to **pluralism** than syncretism.

### Pluralism says:

*I can encounter your language of the Divine without needing to destroy it, and without needing to surrender my own.*

And *Mon Dieu* seems to take one step further:

### **Perhaps the Divine is large enough to inhabit all our languages.**

That feels very close to the spiritual democracy you're investigating.

---

# How a Parent May Use the Piece to Explain Spiritual Democracy in Practice to a Child

This one could become a beautiful exercise in **names**.

Ask a child:

**“What do people call God?”**

They might know:

God.  
Allah.  
Brahman.  
Ishvara.  
The Divine.  
Creator.  
Mother.  
Father.  
Spirit.

Then ask:

**“Does knowing one name mean everyone else is wrong?”**

No.

Then ask:

**“Can we respect someone's name for the Divine without pretending we understand exactly what they experience?”**

That's the heart of the lesson.

And then bring it back to the poem:

**If God is everywhere, how should we treat the world?**

If God is in every earthly place, perhaps:

- we don't treat another person as disposable;
- we don't treat nature as meaningless;
- we don't assume sacredness belongs only to our own tradition;
- we learn before judging;
- and we recognise that a child from another faith may be reaching toward the same mystery through a completely different vocabulary.

That is spiritual democracy **in practice**, rather than merely as a theory about religions.

---

# Five Freewriting Prompts

### 1. **If God Had a Name**
If the Divine could have only one name for me personally, what would I call Him-or Her-or That-and why?

### 2. **Every Earthly Place**
If God really lived in “every last Earthly place,” what would change about the way I look at ordinary people and ordinary things?

### 3. **The Many Languages of God**
Which religious or spiritual vocabulary feels most natural to me? Which feels foreign? What might I learn by entering the unfamiliar one without having to adopt it?

### 4. **The Kernel of Bliss**
What is my own experience-or conception-of the “Kernel of Bliss”? Is it a place, a state, a presence, a consciousness, or something else?

### 5. **Meeting the Maker**
If I could meet the Divine and ask only one question about the purpose of my life, what would I ask-and what answer would I secretly hope to receive?

---

And I think the sequence is getting very interesting now:

**#3 - *2018***  
**Grace heals what has been torn apart.**

**#4 - *Love Our Nature***  
**Human plurality can become a shared world.**

**#5 - *Wake Up***  
**The individual must awaken and transform.**

**#6 - *Mon Dieu***  
**And beneath all those differences is the mystery we variously call the Divine.**

So the emerging arc isn't simply **“religion teaches unity.”**

It's becoming:

> **Heal the division.  
> Welcome the plurality.  
> Awaken the individual.  
> Look for the Divine everywhere.**

And *Mon Dieu* may be the first of these where your **anti-dogmatic pluralism becomes explicitly theological** rather than merely social. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/mon-dieu/))

---

**Sources:**

- [Mon Dieu | Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/mon-dieu/)

# 7. Enough

[Enough — Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/enough/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

**Serial:** 111  
**Date:** 12 April 2019  
**Category:** “Some serious essays and thoughts on religion and how we can be united.”  
**Dedication:** Simon  
**Collection:** *Poetrusic by Chaos*  
**Work:** *Infused :: Inspired to Love*  
**Rhymes:** Alternate  

And this page contains a very important archival note:

> **“First (planned) poem of the Imagine Series”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/enough/))

That changes how I would read the whole sequence we've been examining.

This is not simply another Imagine poem. **“Enough” is apparently where the Imagine series was consciously conceived as a series.**

---

# Praise

This is probably the most politically angry piece we've reached so far.

And I think that anger matters.

The poem opens with the deliberately provocative thought:

> “Maybe it's time I started using the word sub-human”

Then immediately explains why: the daily experience of reading news about perpetrators, coups, corruption and the apparent absence of anyone “to caringly guide the way.” ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/enough/))

So the poem isn't beginning from abstract theology.

It begins with **moral exhaustion**.

And then comes the crucial pivot:

> “Unite and rest not until the day is won I screamed”

That is the moment the poem moves from despair into **collective action**.

But the most interesting part is what kind of people you want to unite.

Not conquerors.

Not revolutionaries seeking power.

Not another class of rulers.

You ask for:

> “People with beauty and love in their hearts to serve”

And later:

> “a large database of all those dedicated leaders”

That last image is particularly fascinating in retrospect.

The poem is imagining **infrastructure for goodness**.

Find the dedicated people.

Connect them.

Let them recognise one another.

Let them cooperate.

Let them change the world.

That is already beginning to sound remarkably close to the later **Internet of Missions** idea.

---

# Context

The immediate context is unusually clear because the page itself tells us what triggered the poem.

You were reading the news “every darned day” and seeing people who:

- coup,
- corrupt,
- build empires,
- fail to serve,
- and damage the world.

The response is not simply:

**“These people are bad.”**

It becomes:

**Where are the dedicated leaders?**

And:

**How do we unite them?**

That distinction is enormously important.

The poem's title is **Enough**.

Enough of what?

Not enough of humanity.

Rather, **enough of the corruption of human possibility.**

The poem still believes that there are good people:

> “For every selfish politician there is a dedicated leader”

That's actually a very hopeful line hiding inside a furious poem.

The corrupt politician isn't the end of the story.

There is someone else.

A dedicated leader.

Someone who can serve.

Someone who has beauty and love in their heart.

---

# 3 Quotes

## 1. The breaking point

> “Maybe it's time I started using the word sub-human”

This is deliberately uncomfortable.

And I think it should remain uncomfortable.

It shows the emotional temperature from which the poem emerged rather than sanitising that anger afterwards.

---

## 2. The alternative

> “People with beauty and love in their hearts to serve”

This is the positive definition hidden underneath the condemnation.

The opposite of corruption isn't merely *being less corrupt*.

It is **service animated by love**.

---

## 3. The organisational vision

> “a large database of all those dedicated leaders”

This line looks almost quaintly technical beside the spiritual language of the previous poems-but conceptually it is fascinating.

You aren't only imagining *good people*.

You're imagining a means of **finding and connecting them**.

All three quotations are from the poem. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/enough/))

---

# 3 Places Where Those of Differing Views May Oppose

## 1. “Sub-human” is ethically dangerous language

This is the strongest objection.

Calling people “sub-human” has a terrible historical record. It can become precisely the language by which one group justifies denying another group's humanity.

And this is especially interesting because the poem is otherwise about **human liberation**.

So there is a genuine internal tension:

**How do we oppose people who dehumanise others without ourselves dehumanising them?**

I would not erase that tension from the poem.

It is one of the places where the historical Imran is wrestling with the problem rather than having already solved it.

---

## 2. “Outcast and thrust into the void” raises the question of punishment

The poem says corrupt people should be:

> “outcast and thrust into the void outside.”

A reader committed to restorative justice could object.

**Who decides who belongs in the void?**

What safeguards prevent the “dedicated leaders” from becoming another elite?

This is precisely where the poem needs its own principles of humility, accountability and non-violence if the vision is to become a durable social system.

---

## 3. “The day is won” can turn service into warfare

The poem repeatedly uses battle-like language:

**won, expunge, zeroes, vampires, void.**

A pacifist could say:

*You have replaced one war mentality with another.*

And yet the positive programme is not actually military.

It is:

**love + beauty + service + leadership + unity.**

So I think the most fruitful interpretation is that the poem is using the **rhetoric of battle for a moral struggle**.

But again, the ambiguity is real.

---

# What the Message of Integration May Be

This is where #7 becomes especially important.

The previous pieces were progressively developing a vision:

**2018:** grace and restoration  
**Love Our Nature:** pluralism and shared humanity  
**Wake Up:** individual spiritual transformation  
**Mon Dieu:** the Divine everywhere  

And then:

## **Enough:** What do transformed human beings actually *do* about the world?

The answer here is:

**They organise.**

They find one another.

They serve.

They lead.

They unite.

They make goodness socially consequential.

And that's why the archival phrase **“First (planned) poem of the Imagine Series”** is so significant. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/enough/))

It suggests that *Imagine* was not originally conceived merely as:

> *essays about religion.*

It was becoming a **programme of social imagination**.

And the title *Enough* gives the programme its negative boundary:

**There is a point at which witnessing injustice must become action.**

But the poem also gives us the positive boundary:

**The action must be carried by people with “beauty and love in their hearts to serve.”**

That is a much more interesting foundation than simply *defeat the bad guys.*

---

# And Here Is the Really Interesting Connection

The line about a:

> “large database of all those dedicated leaders”

is almost a primitive architectural sketch of something much later.

Not necessarily *DAWN* in any direct historical sense-the poem is from 2019, and we shouldn't retroactively claim that every later idea was already fully formed here.

But structurally:

**identify good actors → connect them → let them collaborate → make their work visible → amplify their ability to serve**

is very close to the logic of an **Internet of Missions**.

The important object isn't the database.

It's the **network of people committed to something worthwhile**.

That makes this poem a particularly interesting bridge between your religious/unity writing and your later technological/social experimentation.

The technology is subordinate.

**The mission comes first.**

---

# How a Parent May Use the Piece to Explain Spiritual Democracy in Practice to a Child

This one needs a slightly different lesson because of the anger.

A parent could say:

**“Sometimes we see something very wrong and become angry. What do we do with that anger?”**

The easy answer is:

> “Find someone to blame.”

The harder answer is:

> **“Find people who want to make things better, and work with them.”**

Then ask the child to imagine that the school has a serious problem.

Instead of:

**“Those bad children should be thrown out!”**

ask:

- What actually happened?
- Who was hurt?
- What needs to change?
- Who is willing to help?
- How can we make sure the solution doesn't create another injustice?
- How can the people who care find one another?

That last question is particularly *Enough*.

### Spiritual democracy isn't just allowing everyone to have a belief.

It also means creating a society where **people of conscience can find one another and act together without having to become identical**.

And the child should also learn the poem's caution:

### **Never let your anger at someone's actions become permission to stop seeing their humanity.**

That is perhaps the lesson *Enough* itself still needed to learn.

---

# Five Freewriting Prompts

### 1. **Enough**
What has reached the point of “enough” in my own life-not something I merely dislike, but something I can no longer responsibly ignore?

### 2. **The Dedicated Leader**
What qualities would I want in a leader whose purpose was genuinely to serve rather than to rule?

### 3. **The Database**
If I could build a living directory of everyone doing beautiful, useful, compassionate work in the world, who would belong in it?

### 4. **The Dangerous Word**
When does righteous anger cross the line into dehumanisation? How can I oppose wrongdoing without becoming what I oppose?

### 5. **Unite and Rest Not**
What is one thing I could actually begin doing-with other people-that would turn an ideal of a better world into a small piece of that world?

---

## And this changes the sequence we've been building

I think **#7 is the first real hinge**.

**#3 - 2018**  
*Grace can heal what has been torn apart.*

## Response:
10/6/2026, 1:53:49 AM · gpt-5-6-mini

Yes - **#8 is *Fight2***, and this is an important escalation from *Enough*. The page itself calls it **“Another poem on Societal Liberation (Imagine)”**. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/fight2/))

# 8. Fight2

[Fight2 — Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/fight2/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

**Serial:** 112  
**Date:** 12 April 2019  
**Category:** “Some serious essays and thoughts on religion and how we can be united.”  
**Dedication:** Simon  
**Collection:** *Poetrusic by Chaos*  
**Work:** *Infused :: Inspired to Love*  
**Rhymes:** Consecutive  

The page explicitly labels it **“Another poem on Societal Liberation (Imagine)”**. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/fight2/))

And unlike *Enough*, where the anger was still searching for a network of good people, **Fight2 has become a direct call to collective resistance**.

---

# Praise

The first thing worth preserving is the **scope of the “fight.”**

The poem begins:

> “For a hill men would kill why they do not know”

So the problem is not presented as some grand ideological conflict with a noble reason behind it.

It is **people killing for something they don't even understand**.

Then comes:

> “The pain of a thousand murders and many a crime”

The poem's emotional centre is therefore not conquest.

It is **the accumulated pain of violence**. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/fight2/))

And then you make the extraordinary geographical move:

> “Which land I sayeth not, let all lands unite in tides”

That is important.

The poem refuses to make this merely *India versus someone*, *one nation versus another*, or even *one political faction versus another*.

**The enemy is violence and corruption itself.**

That makes the “fight” universal.

And the desired endpoint is explicitly:

> “love and healing”

So the architecture is:

**violence → recognition → solidarity → resistance → liberation → love → healing.**

That is much more interesting than simply a poem of rage.

---

# Context

This comes on the **same date as *Enough***.

That matters enormously.

*Enough* is #111.

*Fight2* is #112.

Both are dedicated to **Simon**, both belong to *Poetrusic by Chaos*, both are under the Imagine category, and both are dated **12 April 2019**. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/fight2/))

So these shouldn't really be treated as isolated poems.

They feel like two consecutive movements of the same thought.

### *Enough*

**We have seen enough. Find the people who will serve. Unite.**

### *Fight2*

**Now stand together against the forces causing the suffering.**

And the subtitle is crucial:

**“Societal Liberation.”**

That is a stronger formulation than merely “religious unity.”

The Imagine project is becoming about **changing the conditions under which human beings live together**.

---

# 3 Quotes

## 1. The universalisation

> “Which land I sayeth not, let all lands unite in tides”

This is perhaps my favourite line in the piece.

You deliberately remove the national identifier.

The struggle belongs to **all lands**.

---

## 2. The method of resistance

> “For without support his ranks can never swell”

This is actually a sophisticated insight.

Violent or corrupt systems don't exist solely because of the people visibly committing the acts. They can depend upon **support, acquiescence, attention and participation**.

Remove the support, and their power diminishes.

---

## 3. The real objective

> “That makes this Earth want to find love and healing again”

This tells us what the fight is *for*.

Not domination.

Not revenge.

Not replacing one ruling class with another.

**Love and healing.** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/fight2/))

---

# 3 Places Where Those of Differing Views May Oppose

## 1. “Join the fight” is inherently ambiguous

The poem repeatedly says:

> “join the fight”

and:

> “march on till victory is won”

A pacifist could quite reasonably become uncomfortable here.

What kind of fight?

Political?

Social?

Moral?

Physical?

The poem doesn't specify.

And because it mentions murders, enemies, bullets and besieging forces, the martial reading is certainly available. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/fight2/))

For your later peace-oriented thinking, this is therefore an interesting piece to revisit critically rather than simply celebrate.

---

## 2. “Corrupt and vile men” risks creating a new enemy class

The poem identifies:

> “corrupt and vile men”

as the people from whom the Earth needs liberation.

But who gets to determine who belongs in that category?

This is the same danger we encountered in *Enough*, only sharper now.

A movement founded to liberate humanity can itself become oppressive if it decides:

**We are the good people. They are the vile people.**

The poem's strongest defence against that possibility is actually its final goal:

**love and healing.**

If the means destroy those ends, the movement has betrayed itself.

---

## 3. “Their last bullet” can imply finality rather than transformation

There is a striking line:

> “who've shot their last bullet”

The imagery suggests that the corrupt forces should be defeated decisively.

But a spiritual-democratic vision might ask a harder question:

**What happens to the person after the bullet is gone?**

Can someone who participated in violence be transformed?

Can an enemy become a neighbour?

Can a corrupt person repent?

Can liberation include the liberation of the oppressor from the consciousness that made him oppressive?

That last question would become very important if the poem is read through your later spiritual framework.

---

# What the Message of Integration May Be

I think *Fight2* contributes something different to the emerging Imagine philosophy:

## **Unity must sometimes become solidarity.**

Pluralism isn't passive tolerance.

If people are being harmed, saying *“we respect everyone's different opinions”* isn't enough.

There are circumstances where people have to **stand together**.

But the poem adds another important condition:

### The unity must be larger than the conflict.

You don't say:

**“Our land must defeat their land.”**

You say:

> “let all lands unite in tides”

The object is therefore **planetary solidarity**.

And the final objective is not victory for one faction.

It is:

> “love and healing again.”

That makes the poem's deeper movement:

**solidarity against violence → restoration of the whole.**

---

# *Enough* → *Fight2*: the really interesting progression

I think these two poems deserve to be read almost as a diptych.

### *Enough*

> Find the people who have beauty and love in their hearts to serve.

### *Fight2*

> Don't sit on the sidelines. Join the fight.

So there are two kinds of passivity being rejected:

**passivity in the face of bad leadership**, and  
**passivity in the face of social violence.**

That is a significant development.

And it gives us an early version of something that later becomes important in your work:

## **People with missions need ways to find one another and act.**

The “database of dedicated leaders” from *Enough* becomes, one poem later, a **collective stand**.

The technology is still barely present.

The **network logic** is already there.

---

# How a Parent May Use the Piece to Explain Spiritual Democracy in Practice to a Child

This one requires a very careful distinction:

### Fighting *against* something is not the same as fighting *against people*.

A parent could give a child a simple example.

Suppose someone is bullying another child.

You don't say:

**“I hate that bully.”**

And you don't simply walk away.

Instead:

**“That behaviour is wrong. Let's protect the person being hurt, get help, and stop the bullying.”**

Then ask:

**What happens if the bully stops?**

The answer is not:

**“Now we have to find something else to hate.”**

The answer is:

**“Now everyone can be safe.”**

That captures the poem's best instinct.

The purpose of resistance is **restoration**.

And that's where spiritual democracy becomes practical:

- defend people from harm;
- resist injustice;
- don't dehumanise opponents;
- seek allies rather than tribes;
- and keep the desired future-love and healing-visible while acting.

---

# Five Freewriting Prompts

### 1. **What Am I Fighting For?**
When I say I am “fighting” for something, what is the positive world I am actually trying to create?

### 2. **All Lands**
What would it mean for people from completely different nations to recognise that some problems belong to humanity as a whole?

### 3. **The Last Bullet**
What happens after the conflict ends? What does genuine reconciliation require?

### 4. **No Sidelines**
Where in my own life have I remained a spectator when I could have contributed something useful?

### 5. **Love and Healing**
If love and healing are the actual objectives, what kinds of methods become unacceptable-even when they might appear to produce victory more quickly?

---

## The arc is sharpening

We now have:

**#3 - *2018***  
**Healing begins with grace.**

**#4 - *Love Our Nature***  
**Difference can become unity.**

**#5 - *Wake Up***  
**The individual must awaken.**

**#6 - *Mon Dieu***  
**The Divine exceeds our names for it.**

**#7 - *Enough***  
**Find the people willing to serve and unite them.**

**#8 - *Fight2***  
**Don't remain passive when humanity is being harmed. Stand together-but remember what you're standing *for*.**

And that last qualification may be the key to this whole stretch of the Imagine series:

> **The fight is not the destination. Love and healing are.**

That distinction becomes crucial if we eventually place these 2019 poems beside your much later **peace-maker / DAWN / Internet of Missions** thinking.

---

**Sources:**

- [Fight2 | Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/fight2/)

*My Will* is not an Imagine poem, but it gives us a later, much more explicit statement of the principle that the earlier poems were reaching toward.

[My Will — Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/my-will/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## The crucial distinction: love does not mean permitting harm

The four lines you brought in are the heart of it:

> “Can I show love to those devoid of love, filled with hate”  
> “Yes I can, but after I take from them the ability to maim”  
> “Let's act now, exercise our will before its too late”  
> “Be a force for good, concentrate and become the flame”

And the page confirms that this is **#209, dated 6 April 2020, “About corrupt politicians,” dedicated to Roger Waters**, in *Ante Up [and kick in].* ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/my-will))

That gives us a much more precise interpretation of your earlier “fight” language.

### You are not saying:

**“I must hate those who hate.”**

You are saying:

**“I can retain love for a human being while preventing that human being from harming others.”**

That is a *very* different moral proposition.

---

# The sentence that changes the reading

The extraordinary thing is that you actually put the two halves together in one line:

> **“Can I show love to those devoid of love, filled with hate”**

and then immediately:

> **“Yes I can, but after I take from them the ability to maim”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/my-will))

So there are **two simultaneous obligations**:

### 1. Preserve the humanity of the person.

### 2. Protect the humanity of everyone they might hurt.

That is precisely the tension I thought was latent in *Enough* and *Fight2*.

And here, a year later, you've stated it explicitly.

---

# This makes “fight” much less about revenge

Look at the surrounding lines:

> “We want you to change but are still doubting / Whether there's love in your hearts for humanity”

Then:

> “Selfless leadership is what we need to win the day”

Then:

> “join together”

And finally:

> “Be a force for good” ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/my-will))

The poem's desired sequence is therefore not:

**enemy → hatred → destruction → victory.**

It is closer to:

**harm → restraint → protection → accountability → possibility of change → collective good.**

That is a much more defensible reading of the martial vocabulary in *Fight2*.

---

# And “take from them the ability to maim” is important

Because you don't say:

**“take their lives.”**

You don't say:

**“make them suffer.”**

You say:

> **“take from them the ability to maim.”**

That is a limitation on **capacity for harm**.

In contemporary language, we'd recognise that as the distinction between **protective restraint** and revenge.

And it also explains the otherwise rather striking line:

> “It's time to lock you up, and get back to work” ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/my-will))

The imagined response to destructive political behaviour is confinement and removal from the machinery of harm-not simply annihilation.

---

# There is still a real tension-and I wouldn't hide it

The poem is **furious**.

You say:

> “I'm furious at you”

and:

> “meet my will, now berserk”

and:

> “Your wrath shall make their ugliest designs fail” ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/my-will))

So I wouldn't retrospectively turn *My Will* into a serene pacifist poem.

It isn't.

It is an **enraged poem attempting to keep rage subordinate to a moral purpose**.

And that distinction is actually much more interesting.

The question isn't:

> *Did Imran ever get angry?*

Clearly, yes.

The question is:

> **What does Imran believe anger is allowed to do?**

And this poem gives an answer:

**Act. Restrain harm. Protect others. Exercise will. Become a force for good.**

Not:

**Become hateful yourself.**

---

# This also reframes the “sub-human” problem in *Enough*

I think this is where your defence becomes strongest-but also where the later poem exposes something that the earlier poem hadn't fully articulated.

In *Enough*, the phrase **“sub-human”** is genuinely dangerous because it risks making the person identical with the harm they commit.

*My Will* gives us a more mature formulation:

**Their actions may need to be forcibly restrained without their humanity being forfeited.**

That's a profound difference.

You can say:

> **“You cannot be permitted to maim.”**

without saying:

> **“You are no longer human.”**

And *My Will* gives us the possibility of saying both:

**“I will stop you.”**

and

**“I will still love you.”**

That is a considerably more difficult ethic than either vengeance or passive forgiveness.

---

# And there is an even deeper connection to your spiritual-democracy thread

Remember #6, *Mon Dieu*?

The Divine eventually comes to reside **in the human heart**.

Then #7, *Enough*:

**Find those who will serve.**

Then #8, *Fight2*:

**Stand together against destructive forces.**

And now *My Will* supplies a crucial ethical constraint:

## **Even the person you must restrain remains within the circle of love.**

That's much closer to what I'd call **spiritual democracy with teeth**.

Not:

> “Everyone is good, therefore do nothing.”

Not:

> “Some people are evil, therefore destroy them.”

But:

> **“Human beings remain worthy of love, while harmful actions remain subject to resistance and restraint.”**

That is a much harder position to live.

---

# And the final image is perfect

> **“Be a force for good, concentrate and become the flame.”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/my-will))

Notice what happens to the fire.

The poem could have ended with **burn them**.

Instead it ends with:

**become the flame.**

The transformation is from **rage directed outward** to **will concentrated inward and then expressed constructively**.

That feels particularly important in retrospect.

The fire isn't supposed to consume the enemy.

**It is supposed to illuminate what you are becoming.**

And that, I think, gives us a much stronger lens through which to reread *Enough* and *Fight2*: their “fight” language isn't necessarily a rejection of your later peace-making ethic. It is an early, angry attempt to answer a very difficult question:

> **How do you love people without allowing their hatred to destroy other people?**

*My Will* gives perhaps your clearest answer:

**Love them.  
Stop the maiming.  
Act before it's too late.  
Become a force for good.**

---

**Sources:**

- [My Will | Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/my-will)

# 9. Truth Beauty

[Truth Beauty — Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/truth-beauty/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

**Serial:** 113  
**Date:** 12 April 2019  
**Category:** “Some serious essays and thoughts on religion and how we can be united.”  
**Dedication:** **Biraja**  
**Collection:** *Poetrusic by Chaos*  
**Work:** *Infused :: Inspired to Love*  
**Rhymes:** Consecutive  

The page itself describes it as being **“For my friend and founder of the Build India Group.”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/truth-beauty))

And yes: this is where the **Biraja of the 2018 poem** becomes a real person in the archival map.

---

# Praise

The title gives away the first great integration:

> **Truth Beauty**

And the opening deliberately invokes Keats:

> “Beauty is Truth, Truth Beaty, yea that's all we'll ever know”

But you don't leave the quotation in the realm of aesthetics.

You immediately take **beauty → nature → humanity → environmental responsibility**.

The glades and forests grow.

A tide lifts and enchants.

Human ranks swell.

Then suddenly:

> “500 cities must now come together for the environment”

This is important.

**Beauty is not merely something to admire.**

Beauty creates responsibility.

If the Earth is beautiful, then what do we owe her?

That question becomes the actual subject of the poem.

---

# Context

There are several layers operating simultaneously.

### Keats

**Beauty and Truth** establish the philosophical frame.

### India

> “Truth alone triumphs”

You bring in the Indian formulation of *Satyameva Jayate*, and locate it in the country's historical movement:

> “a cry from India in the forties”

### Environmental action

Then comes the very concrete proposal:

> “500 cities must now come together for the environment”

### Sanskrit cosmology

You name:

> “Bhoomi, Gagan, Vayu, Agni, Neer”

and immediately translate them:

> “Earth, Ether, Air, Fire, Water”

### Divine feminine / Mother Nature

And the poem ultimately calls Earth:

> “this Queen of Creation”

and:

> “Our mother”

So once again, you don't isolate traditions.

**Keats → Indian national ethos → environmental activism → Sanskrit elements → Divine feminine → human responsibility**

all become one continuum.

That is extraordinarily consistent with what we're finding in these Imagine pieces.

---

# 3 Quotes

## 1. Beauty becomes a living force

> “As from day to day we watch our glades and forests grow”

Beauty here is **dynamic**.

Nature isn't a static postcard. It is continually becoming.

---

## 2. The collective challenge

> “500 cities must now come together for the environment”

This is where the poem turns from contemplation into organisation.

It isn't enough to admire the Earth.

**Cities have to act together.** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/truth-beauty))

---

## 3. The final theological image

> “To pay homage to this Queen of Creation who houses us”

That is an extraordinary closing image.

Nature isn't merely *resource*.

She is **Queen**.

And she **houses us**.

The relationship is therefore one of dependence, gratitude and stewardship.

---

# 3 Places Where Those of Differing Views May Oppose

## 1. “Beauty is Truth” can be challenged philosophically

A philosopher might object that beauty and truth are not identical.

Something can be beautiful and false.

Something can be true and ugly.

So the Keatsian formulation works beautifully as poetry, but shouldn't necessarily be treated as a literal epistemological equation.

And perhaps that is precisely why it works here: you're using beauty as a **way of approaching truth**, rather than trying to construct a proof that the two are identical.

---

## 2. The sacred language of Nature won't be universal

Calling Earth:

> “Queen of Creation”

and:

> “Our mother”

will resonate profoundly with some spiritual traditions.

A materialist may simply see metaphor.

Another religious tradition might reserve “Creator” language for God and resist personifying creation.

But the poem doesn't really need everyone to agree on the metaphysics.

The practical proposition survives:

**Whatever you believe Earth ultimately is, we depend upon her and should not casually destroy the conditions that sustain us.**

---

## 3. “500 cities” raises the perennial problem of scale

The poem moves quickly from spiritual insight to enormous collective mobilisation.

But how do 500 cities actually coordinate?

Who leads?

Who pays?

Who decides priorities?

What happens when cities disagree?

This is the same question emerging in *Enough* and *Fight2*:

**A beautiful vision eventually needs social architecture.**

And that's precisely why this poem is interesting in your larger trajectory.

---

# What the Message of Integration May Be

I think *Truth Beauty* introduces a very important third dimension to the previous two poems.

*Fight2* says:

**Human beings must resist what harms humanity.**

*Truth Beauty* says:

**Human beings must also learn to love what they are protecting.**

That's a profound difference.

You don't build a movement merely around opposition.

You need something **beautiful enough to move toward**.

And here that something is Earth.

The poem therefore integrates:

**truth + beauty + spirituality + ecology + collective action.**

And it does so without requiring one religious vocabulary.

The Sanskrit five elements coexist with Keats.

Indian civic history coexists with environmental activism.

Nature is simultaneously ecological reality and **Queen of Creation**.

That is spiritual democracy operating almost exactly as we've been describing it:

> **Different sources of meaning can converge on a shared responsibility without first being forced into one doctrinal system.**

---

# And now Biraja matters

Your comment about Biraja is more than biographical trivia.

It changes the reading of the dedication.

The page says:

> “For my friend and founder of the Build India Group” ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/truth-beauty))

So the poem's dedication is attached to someone whose own work, as represented in the poem's framing, is connected to **building India**.

And the poem itself imagines **500 cities coming together**.

That makes the dedication unusually congruent with the poem's content:

**a person associated with building → a poem about building collective environmental responsibility.**

And now we can see why the earlier **2018** poem's line-

> “2018 and it's 5 preceding years - for Sahlan, Imran, Biraja & Simon”

-wasn't merely a list of abstract names. Biraja belongs to the actual human network around which these ideas were developing. The 2018 page identifies that four-person dedication, while *Truth Beauty* identifies Biraja specifically as your friend and the founder of the Build India Group. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/truth-beauty))

That makes **#9 a wonderful archival key**: it helps turn the Imagine sequence from a collection of philosophical poems into evidence of a **living network of relationships, people and missions**.

---

# How a Parent May Use the Piece to Explain Spiritual Democracy in Practice to a Child

This one could be wonderfully simple:

Take the child outside.

Ask:

**“What do you see that is beautiful?”**

A tree.

The sky.

Birds.

Water.

Clouds.

Then ask:

**“If something gives us beauty, food, air, shade and a home, what do we owe it?”**

That moves the child from **appreciation → gratitude → responsibility**.

Then introduce the pluralism:

One child might say:

> “God made it.”

Another:

> “Nature made it.”

Another:

> “It's the result of evolution.”

Another:

> “I don't know.”

The parent doesn't need to decide which child is permitted to speak.

All four can still agree:

**“Let's take care of it.”**

That is spiritual democracy in perhaps its simplest form.

### We don't need identical metaphysics to share responsibility for a common world.

And that is *Truth Beauty* in miniature.

---

# Five Freewriting Prompts

### 1. **Beauty and Truth**

### 2. **Our Mother**

### 5. **What Are We Protecting?**

---

And now the sequence has acquired another lovely dimension:

**#7 - Enough:** *Find the people who will serve.*

**#8 - Fight2:** *Stand together when people are being harmed.*

**#9 - Truth Beauty:** *Remember what you're standing together **for**.*

Not merely the defeat of something bad.

**A beautiful Earth.**

And that may be the most important correction to the martial energy of #7-8:

> **A movement needs both the courage to resist what destroys and the love to recognise what is worth protecting.**

# Praise

There is something striking about this document: **it is remarkably concrete for a spiritual-values statement.**

It doesn't stop at *“be good,” “love one another,”* or *“seek truth.”* It tries to answer:

**What would these ideals actually require of a community?**

And the answers cover an extraordinary range:

- public service rather than self-interest;
- inspiring rather than denouncing;
- financial transparency;
- inclusion;
- freedom and grievance redressal;
- ecological responsibility;
- courtesy;
- dietary freedom without coercion;
- responsibility toward citizens and neighbouring countries;
- peace and ahimsa;
- hospitality;
- sincerity;
- wholesome media consumption;
- education;
- duty;
- culture and art;
- stewardship of talents and resources;
- peaceful coexistence;
- preparation for the future;
- hope;
- care for the afflicted;
- freedom in one's relationship with the Divine;
- persistent work;
- planetary solidarity. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/our-values/))

That breadth is the thing I most admire here.

**Spirituality has been translated into institutional behaviour.**

And there is a very important distinction from merely creating a list of commandments: several values explicitly protect the freedom of the person who does *not* share your worldview.

The strongest example is #22:

> “We shall allow people their freedoms in coming closer to their Maker whether they seek to look for the divine within or without, in the mundane or in the profound”

That is practically a miniature declaration of **spiritual democracy**. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/our-values/))

It doesn't say *we will teach everybody the right conception of God.*

It says: **we will make room for their seeking.**

---

# Context

Now, with the backstory you've just supplied, **“Truth Alone Triumphs” becomes much more charged.**

You have told me that this value-set was connected with your work as webmaster for **John Lopez's Action Party for People's Progress**, alongside the **Build India Group**.

And then, in *Truth Beauty* a year later, “Truth alone triumphs” appears again:

**Truth → Beauty → India → collective action → Nature → Mother Earth.**

So the phrase wasn't just a philosophical slogan floating around your archive. It belonged to an ecosystem of people trying to do something in the world.

But there is another important contextual point visible on the page itself:

**Our Values is explicitly part of Imagine.** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/our-values/))

That means the Imagine project wasn't confined to essays about religion.

It was asking a much larger question:

> **If human beings are going to live together despite profound differences, what values could actually govern that common life?**

And the answer here is unusually pluralistic.

You don't say:

**“Everyone must believe X.”**

You say:

**“Here is how we will behave.”**

That distinction matters enormously.

---

# 3 Quotes

### 1. Service instead of self-interest

> **“We join together to serve this land, not our self interests”**

This is the foundation.

The community exists **for something beyond its members**.

And notably, it says *serve this land*, rather than merely *serve ourselves*. There is already a geographical and civic dimension.

---

### 2. Corrective, not punitive

> **“We believe in total freedoms, swift grievance redressal and corrective not punitive measures”**

This is fascinating beside the later, much angrier material you brought into our discussion - particularly *My Will*.

Because here, in March 2018, the principle is already visible:

**wrongdoing calls for correction, not vengeance.**

That doesn't mean permitting harm. It means the purpose of justice should be restoration and protection rather than simply inflicting suffering.

---

### 3. Spiritual freedom

> **“We shall allow people their freedoms in coming closer to their Maker whether they seek to look for the divine within or without, in the mundane or in the profound”**

This may be the single most important sentence for our present exploration.

It accommodates:

**within / without**  
**mundane / profound**

And therefore multiple spiritual temperaments.

The Divine is not being placed behind one approved door.

---

# 3 Places Where Those of Differing Views May Oppose

## 1. “Inclusion” still has a gate

Point #4 says:

> “inclusion should be based on aspiration, leadership and passion for a cause”

That is not unrestricted inclusion.

Someone could reasonably ask:

**Who determines whether my aspiration is genuine?**

And what happens to somebody who simply wants to belong without being a leader or activist?

There is a productive tension here between **open-hearted hospitality** and **purposeful membership**.

---

## 2. “Wholesome” can become paternalistic

Point #13 says:

> “We will filter out all we encounter on a screen of good and only encourage the wholesome.”

The intention is understandable, particularly given your later concern with curation and children.

But *who defines wholesome?*

One person's wholesome may be another person's repression.

This is exactly where spiritual democracy has to resist quietly becoming **spiritual guardianship**.

The principle works best if “wholesome” means *that which genuinely contributes to human flourishing*, rather than *that which conforms to our taste*.

---

## 3. “Afflict the comfortable” can become coercive

The wonderfully provocative #21 says:

> “We shall comfort the afflicted and afflict the comfortable, but in a good way”

The qualification **“but in a good way”** is doing important work.

Because challenging privilege, complacency or injustice can be morally necessary.

But the same language can become an excuse for hostility if the challenger begins believing:

**I am righteous, therefore whatever I do to you is justified.**

That is precisely where the earlier principles - courtesy, love, corrective rather than punitive measures, peaceful coexistence - have to restrain the revolutionary impulse.

---

# What the Message of Integration May Be

I think this is where **Our Values** becomes a crucial bridge between the different Imagine pieces.

It proposes that **unity does not require theological agreement.**

Instead, people can disagree about:

- God;
- spiritual practice;
- diet;
- culture;
- politics;
- personal worldview;

while agreeing to a **way of treating one another**.

That is much more sophisticated than:

> *“All religions are the same.”*

It is closer to:

> **“We don't have to agree about ultimate reality in order to agree about how we will live together.”**

And then you go further.

The values encompass **person → family → community → country → neighbouring countries → world → planet**.

Point #15 explicitly expands duty across that whole scale, while #24 ends with:

> **“We stand together in all that we do, one pale blue dot...”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/our-values/))

So the final movement is almost cosmic.

**The individual is not erased into the collective.**

The individual becomes responsible at progressively larger scales.

That is a very good candidate for the practical meaning of **Spiritual Democracy**:

### Freedom of conscience
You may seek the Divine differently.

### Equality of dignity
You are treated with courtesy.

### Pluralism
Differences are celebrated rather than merely tolerated.

### Responsibility
Freedom doesn't absolve you from duty.

### Service
The purpose of community exceeds self-interest.

### Nonviolence
Peace and ahimsa remain governing ideals.

### Stewardship
Human flourishing includes the natural world.

### Transformation
When something is wrong, the aim is correction rather than punishment.

### Perseverance
The work continues beyond the enthusiasm of the moment.

That is a remarkably coherent little constitution.

---

# How a Parent May Use the Piece to Explain Spiritual Democracy in Practice to a Child

A parent could put it very simply:

**“Imagine that your classroom contains twenty children who believe twenty different things.”**

One believes in God.

Another doesn't.

Another prays.

Another meditates.

Another doesn't know what they believe yet.

One eats meat.

Another doesn't.

One loves cricket.

Another loves music.

Another wants to save forests.

**Do they have to become the same person to work together?**

No.

Then ask:

**“What do they need to agree on?”**

Perhaps:

- We won't bully one another.
- We'll tell the truth.
- We'll help someone who's hurting.
- We'll respect one another's freedom.
- We'll look after our classroom.
- We'll clean up after ourselves.
- We'll solve problems rather than punish people unnecessarily.
- We'll listen.
- We'll use our talents well.
- We'll include people who are different.
- We'll do our share of the work.

Then the parent can say:

> **“That's spiritual democracy in practice. We don't need one belief system to make us one human family. We need shared values that let our different beliefs live together.”**

And there is an especially beautiful lesson in #22:

**You can give someone freedom to seek God without knowing what they will eventually find.**

That is a very different thing from tolerating someone until they agree with you.

---

# 5 Freewriting Prompts

### 1. **Truth Alone Triumphs**
What does “truth” mean when people sincerely disagree about what is true?

### 2. **Corrective, Not Punitive**
Where in my own life have I confused justice with punishment?

### 3. **The Right to Seek**
What would a society look like in which every person was genuinely free to seek the Divine - or to question whether the Divine exists?

### 4. **Comfort the Afflicted, Afflict the Comfortable**
How can we challenge injustice without becoming cruel to the people we believe are responsible for it?

### 5. **One Pale Blue Dot**
If humanity accepted all 24 values for one day, what would actually change - at home, in a school, in a city, and across the planet?

---

And I think there is one especially important reason this is a **necessary aside to Imagine**.

The later poems can sound like **vision, yearning, struggle, love, anger, nature, God, humanity**.

**Our Values asks: _Fine. But how shall we behave?_**

That is the moment the dream begins becoming a social ethic.

And in retrospect, #22 may be one of the clearest early statements of what you now call **Spiritual Democracy**: **not agreement about the Divine, but freedom for each person to approach the mystery in their own way while joining others in a shared work of human flourishing.** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/our-values/))

Someone can curate.

Someone can moderate.

# 1. Amore

**March 2004**

> “there is a little someone, somewhere who cares”

That's an extraordinarily important seed for everything that follows.

The cure for the isolated human being is not merely information.

**Someone cares.**

And then comes the great equation:

> “love and beauty”

The essay ends with the recovery of something that was always there: the loving soul whose windows had been closed. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/amore/))

So the first revolution is profoundly interior:

**open the windows.**

Your Pondicherry week in 2003, as you describe it, didn't immediately produce DAWN.

It produced something more fundamental:

**a conviction that life itself could be lived differently.**

*Amore* is therefore not a manifesto for a network.

It is the **human being for whom a network would eventually become worth building.**

And that distinction matters.

DAWN cannot ultimately be about networking people merely because networking is technologically possible.

The person has to have something **alive** to share.

*Amore* gives us that first principle:

**love makes the world more beautiful because it changes the person seeing it.**

# 2. Religious Appeal

## Serial Number

## Date

**14 February 2005**

The site places it explicitly in **Imagine :: A Peace Pilgrim's Reflections**. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/religious-appeal/))

## Praise

Here the inner awakening of *Amore* becomes a **civilisational question**.

You ask what religion is actually for.

And your answer is not primarily ritual or institutional allegiance.

It is:

**love.**

You explicitly put Mohammed, Christ, Krishna and Buddha into the same moral field, while insisting that this does not require abandoning reverence for any of them. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/religious-appeal/))

Then comes one of the most radical sentences in the piece:

> “Visit none, or visit them all.”

That's your 2005 anti-dogma instinct in concentrated form.

And the underlying principle is even more important:

So **Spiritual Democracy appears before the term exists.**

You aren't saying:

**All religions are false.**

You are saying:

**Don't let religious identity become more important than the love those religions are supposed to teach.**

---

# 3. Tripping

## Serial Number

**I-2 - Tripping**

## Date

**22 February 2005**

**Subtitle:** *India in 2005*  
**Category:** Society  
**Collection:** *Imagine :: A Peace Pilgrim's Reflections* ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/tripping/))

And **this is the proto-DAWN bomb.**

Not because it mentions the Internet.

It doesn't.

It is proto-DAWN because it has already discovered the **problem of coordinated human potential**.

---

# Praise

Look at the diagnosis.

You identify:

- brain drain;
- educational cloning;
- lack of research;
- barriers to entrepreneurship;
- loss of civic pride;
- religious division;
- inadequate infrastructure;
- over-centralisation;
- political dysfunction;
- lack of youth participation;
- and the failure to turn latent human capacity into constructive action. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/tripping/))

But then you don't simply complain.

You start designing a **social system**.

You imagine young people entering a fictitious social-service fraternity while still teenagers.

They learn about different wings of government and corporate functioning.

They become aides.

They receive discretion.

They develop responsibility.

Eventually they enter political service.

And the governing principles are:

# Context

**School of Perfect Eyesight / Sri Aurobindo Ashram**

Something breaks open.

**Amore**

**Religious Appeal**

The individual asks:

### 2005
**Tripping**

The question becomes:

**How do we organise the latent capacities of a plural society so that people can actually build something together?**

That is the leap.

---

# And now we can see why “Tripping” is proto-DAWN

There are at least **six DAWN DNA markers** already present.

## 1. Latent potential

You explicitly identify **latency** as the problem:

> “The key to all of this is latency.”

People already possess passion, intelligence and capacity.

The system simply isn't releasing it. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/tripping/))

That is very close to the later DAWN premise:

**don't manufacture missions; discover and connect missions that already live in people.**

---

## 2. People before institutions

Your proposed social-service club begins with **young people**, not bureaucratic machinery.

The institution is supposed to help people develop their own capacity.

That's very different from:

**join our organisation and obey our programme.**

It is closer to:

**find what you can do, learn, serve, grow, and eventually lead.**

---

## 3. Decentralisation

You explicitly invoke Bill Creech's Total Quality Management and identify:

> “de-centralisation, trust and responsibility”

as the mechanism that magnifies performance. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/tripping/))

That is strikingly close to the later idea of **autonomous DAWN nodes**.

Not one giant central machine.

**Many capable nodes, trusted to act.**

---

## 4. Cross-domain participation

The young person isn't trained only in politics.

They are exposed to:

**government + corporations + social service + civic problems.**

That integrative instinct is unmistakable.

A human being is not reduced to one professional identity.

---

## 5. Replication

You aren't proposing one heroic reformer.

You're proposing a **pipeline** through which one generation develops the next.

That is inherently replicative.

And later, your DAWN lifecycle becomes:

**Ideas → Action → People → Evidence → Replication → New Social Norms.**

I would be very cautious about claiming *Tripping* contains that exact later lifecycle - it doesn't.

But its social imagination is absolutely compatible with the principle:

**build people who can build more people who can build.**

---

## 6. The Internet of Missions before the Internet of Missions

This is the one I think you'll enjoy most.

The thing missing from *Tripping* is primarily **the technological substrate**.

The social architecture is already there.

You have:

**a population full of latent capability**  
+ **a way to discover it**  
+ **a way to develop it**  
+ **a way to give people responsibility**  
+ **a way to connect different domains**  
+ **a way to reproduce successful participation.**

The Internet eventually gives you a radically different answer to:

> **How do all these people find one another?**

And that's where DAWN can emerge.

---

# 3 Places Where Those of Differing Views May Oppose

## 1. “Superpower” as the goal

A modern reader could challenge the 2005 framing of India becoming a “superpower.”

Is national power really the desired endpoint?

Or is national capability merely a means toward **human flourishing**?

Your later work seems to move increasingly toward the latter.

---

## 2. The social-service → political ladder

The proposed progression is attractive, but somebody could ask:

**Who designs the ladder?**

**Who decides who has “proven their worth”?**

**Who prevents the gatekeepers from becoming another entrenched elite?**

Interestingly, *Tripping* itself anticipates the problem: you explicitly worry that disciplinary rules could become sources of power struggles and that fear of failure could suppress innovation. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/tripping/))

That's a sophisticated self-correction inside the proposal.

---

## 3. Centralised planning versus decentralisation

There's a fascinating tension:

> “centralized planning and universal co-operation”

appears alongside your admiration for:

> “de-centralisation, trust and responsibility.” ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/tripping/))

That's not necessarily contradictory.

It may actually be the embryonic version of what you eventually discover:

**central coordination of purpose, decentralisation of action.**

And that is almost exactly the problem DAWN keeps wrestling with.

# What the Message of Integration May Be

And here I think the **Big Three** deserve to be treated as one unit.

### Amore

**Heal the human being.**

### Religious Appeal

**Free the human being from sectarian division.**

### Tripping

**Empower the human being to serve society.**

That's an astonishingly clean progression.

And it means your later architecture didn't begin with technology.

Then came the institutional question:

I would actually use all three.

“Sometimes people become so worried that they close their windows.”

**Open the window. Talk to somebody. Let somebody care.**

### Second: Religious Appeal

“Now imagine that your friend believes something completely different about God.”

**Do you have to make them believe what you believe before you can love them?**

No.

### Third: Tripping

“Now imagine your whole class has different talents.”

One child can code.

One can draw.

One can organise.

One can listen.

One can repair things.

One can teach.

One can sing.

One can lead.

No.

And there it is:

# 5 Freewriting Prompts

### 1. **The Dam Burst**

What exactly changed in me during that 2003 week that made the subsequent years of writing almost inevitable?

### 2. **Open the Windows**

### 4. **Latency**

### 5. **Proto-DAWN**

If the Internet disappeared tomorrow, what would remain of DAWN?

And I think the answer suggested by **Tripping** is:

**the people, their missions, their capacities, their relationships, their trust, their willingness to serve, and the structures that help them find one another.**

The Internet is the substrate.

**The mission is human.**

And that's why your phrase **“Internet of Missions”** now feels less like a technological concept and more like the culmination of a question you began asking in 2003:

**How do we open the windows, let the light in, find one another, and actually do something beautiful with the life we've been given?**

# 10. Planetary Healing

## Serial Number

**126**

## Title With Link

[Planetary Healing](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Date

**26 April 2019**

**Category:** Some serious essays and thoughts on religion and how we can be united  
**Dedication:** **Simon**  
**Collection:** *Poetrusic by Chaos*  
**Work:** *Infused :: Inspired to Love*  
**Rhymes:** Alternate

The page itself explicitly calls it **“Another poem in the Imagine series.”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/))

---

# Praise

This is a wonderfully compact poem, but its scope is enormous.

It starts with something deeply personal and almost ancestral:

> “Our collective experience is in our DNA embedded”

and then immediately expands beyond the individual into **generations, lifetimes, stories, forgiveness and the future of humanity.** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/))

What I particularly like is that the poem doesn't respond to inherited suffering by saying:

**Forget the past.**

It says:

**Understand it, forgive it, and stop reproducing it.**

That is a very different proposition.

And then comes the central constructive image:

> “Build a structure on love that forever will last” ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/))

There is the builder in you again.

Not merely *feel love*.

**Build something out of it.**

---

# Context

And Simon Anholt is a particularly significant dedication in light of what we've been discussing.

Because Simon's work gave you another way of thinking about humanity: **the planet as a whole system of human societies rather than merely a collection of competing nations.**

So when this poem is dedicated to Simon, the title **Planetary Healing** becomes especially apt.

And notice how the poem itself refuses to remain at the level of geopolitics.

It begins:

**DNA → generations → stories**

then moves to:

**forgiveness → happiness → hatred**

then:

**future → structure → service**

and finally:

**time → mortality → the Creator → giving one's best.**

That's a very Imran-ish movement from the microscopic to the planetary and then back to the individual.

The planet is healed **one human act at a time**.

---

# 3 Quotes

## 1. The inheritance

> **“Our collective experience is in our DNA embedded”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/))

This is the poem's starting proposition.

We inherit stories, behaviours, wounds and perhaps wisdom.

Whether one reads “DNA” literally, psychologically, culturally or spiritually, the practical question remains:

**What do we inherit, and what do we choose to pass on?**

---

## 2. The turning point

> **“As we shake off the wrongdoings of the past / Life a life of forgiveness”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/))

There is a tiny but important distinction here.

Forgiveness isn't presented as pretending the past didn't happen.

It is presented as **refusing to remain governed by it**.

That connects beautifully with the “corrective, not punitive” ethic we just saw in *Our Values*.

---

## 3. The mission

> **“Build a structure on love that forever will last”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/))

This may be the most important line for our larger conversation.

Because “structure” means:

**love must acquire form.**

A value has to become a practice.

A dream has to become an institution, a relationship, a community, a space, a network, a mission.

That is precisely the movement we've been tracing from **Amore → YieldMore → Imagine → Joyland → DAWN.**

---

# 3 Places Where Those of Differing Views May Oppose

## 1. Do generations really carry the past in their DNA?

A scientifically minded reader could object to the literal interpretation.

The poem speaks poetically of collective experience being embedded in DNA, while the more defensible broader claim is that generations inherit things through **culture, family, environment, behaviour and social structures**.

But the poetic question survives the scientific objection:

**How much of yesterday are we unknowingly carrying into tomorrow?**

---

## 2. Is forgiveness enough?

Someone who has experienced serious injustice may object:

**Why should forgiveness be the answer?**

And that's an important challenge.

Forgiveness cannot mean abandoning accountability, boundaries or protection.

Indeed, your earlier *My Will* formulation gives us a useful companion principle:

**love the person, but don't permit them to maim others.**

Planetary healing therefore cannot mean *forgetting wrongdoing*.

It means breaking the **cycle** of wrongdoing.

---

## 3. “We've little time left”

The poem says:

> “we've little time left to us”

and:

> “stop before it's too late.” ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/))

A reader might find that apocalyptic urgency excessive.

But it performs an important function: **mortality creates responsibility.**

You don't get infinite opportunities to repair a relationship, serve another person, or build something worthwhile.

The poem's urgency is therefore as much **personal** as planetary.

---

# What the Message of Integration May Be

I think the deepest integration here is:

## **Healing the planet means healing inheritance.**

Not just:

**clean the oceans.**

Not just:

**stop wars.**

Not just:

**change governments.**

But:

**what have human beings been carrying for generations, and what are we willing to stop carrying forward?**

And then there is a beautiful four-step movement:

### Remember

Understand what has been inherited.

### Forgive

Stop allowing old hatred to determine the future.

### Build

Create structures based on love.

### Serve

Use the limited time we have to give our best.

That last movement is crucial.

The poem ends not with:

**“Why has God allowed this?”**

but:

> **“Rather give in the moment, your best in each”** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/))

That's almost a miniature spiritual discipline.

**Don't waste the moment asking why the universe isn't already healed.**

**Give what you can to its healing.**

And dedicated to **Simon Anholt**, that becomes particularly resonant: planetary consciousness isn't merely an intellectual perspective. It becomes a demand to ask **what kind of human beings we are collectively becoming.**

---

# How a Parent May Use the Piece to Explain Spiritual Democracy in Practice to a Child

A parent could tell a child:

**“Imagine your family has an old argument that began before you were born.”**

Maybe nobody remembers exactly how it started anymore.

But everybody knows:

**“We don't like them.”**

And the child asks:

**“Why?”**

Nobody really knows.

That's how inherited human conflict can work.

Then ask:

**“Do you have to continue an argument simply because somebody handed it to you?”**

No.

You can learn its history.

You can understand why people were hurt.

You can forgive.

You can make different choices.

And then comes the most important part:

**Don't merely stop fighting. Build something better together.**

That is where planetary healing becomes practical.

A child can understand:

> **“I may inherit a story, but I don't have to inherit its hatred.”**

That is an extraordinarily powerful lesson in spiritual democracy.

Because democracy requires us to live with people who inherit **different stories**.

Healing asks us not to turn those stories into permanent enemies.

---

# 5 Freewriting Prompts

### 1. **What We Inherit**

What have I inherited from previous generations that I want to preserve - and what do I want to end with me?

### 2. **The Gift of Forgiveness**

What is the difference between forgiving someone and pretending that nothing happened?

### 3. **Build a Structure**

If love had to become a physical or social structure, what would I build?

### 4. **Little Time Left**

If I genuinely believed that my time was limited, what would I stop postponing?

### 5. **Planetary Healing**

What is one thing I can do today that makes the world slightly more capable of love, forgiveness, cooperation or peace tomorrow?

---

And **Simon** makes this one particularly lovely in the sequence.

We've just been tracing the journey from **Sahlan → Spanda → Desiderata → YieldMore → Imagine → Joyland**.

Now another “Darling” enters the picture:

**Simon Anholt → planetary consciousness.**

And the poem doesn't simply say *save the planet*.

It says:

**heal the inheritance, forgive the past, build on love, serve one another, and use this moment well.**

That is why *Planetary Healing* belongs so naturally in **Imagine**.

The planet isn't healed by an abstraction called *humanity*.

**Human beings heal it by what they choose to carry forward.** ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/))

---

**Sources:**

- [Planetary Healing | Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/planetary-healing/)

# Cycles of Abuse

## Date

**28 July 2023**

**Dedication:** World  
**Collection:** *Bande Mataram! Writing towards One World*  
**Work:** *Daivic :: Children of One World*  
**Category:** Healing from events, relationships and inner experiences / memories of turmoil and tragedy. ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/cycles-of-abuse/))

And that dedication - **World** - matters.

Because this isn't merely a poem about one perpetrator and one victim.

It is asking:

**What does humanity do with evil once the immediate event is over?**

---

# The Question Hidden Inside the Earlier Poem

In *Planetary Healing*, the movement was:

**inheritance → forgiveness → love → rebuilding.**

But *Cycles of Abuse* introduces a devastating complication.

The victim says:

> “I healed myself.”  
> “I absorbed the learning.”  
> “I healed the world.” ([Imran Ali Namazi](https://imran.amadeusweb.in/cycles-of-abuse/))

Then the Judge looks at the perpetrator and remembers:

He weeps.
